Story of black bear taking wounded animals to wildlife center is too good to be true
Story by Emery Winter • 6d • 5 min read


A screenshot of pair of AI-generated images stacked vertically together, the top one showing a black bear carrying a raccoon to the back of a building and the bottom one of a black bear carrying a fox to the back of a very similar, but not exactly the same building. Snopes' red 
A screenshot of pair of AI-generated images stacked vertically together, the top one showing a black bear carrying a raccoon to the back of a building and the bottom one of a black bear carrying a fox to the back of a very similar, but not exactly the same building. Snopes' red

Claim:

As shared in September 2026 social media posts, a black bear took a raccoon and later a fox to a wildlife center where it had previously been treated.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

In September 2026, a rumor circulated online about a black bear that apparently took a wounded raccoon, and later a wounded fox, to a wildlife center where the bear itself had been treated.

For example, one Facebook post (archived) said:

The first time, nobody believed it. Security cameras at a wildlife center captured a black bear approaching a rear entrance carrying a wounded raccoon in its mouth. It placed the animal gently beside the door. Then disappeared into the woods.

[…]

Several weeks later, cameras recorded a bear approaching a side entrance with an injured fox. Same careful approach. Same gentle placement near the door. Same quiet exit into the trees.

Both animals were found alive and received treatment.

Workers compared the two recordings. Both bears had the same unusual white mark on their chest. Same bear. Two deliveries. Two injured animals brought to the one place that could help them.

Staff searched old photographs and records of bears previously treated at the facility. That's when everything clicked.

Years earlier, the same bear had been rescued, treated at that very wildlife center, and released back into the wild. He had been a patient there. And now he was bringing patients back.

Officials believe the bear remembered where it had once been helped and returned because it somehow understood that injured animals would be safe there.


Other Facebook (archived) posts (archived) shared the same text. The black bear story also appeared in a YouTube short (archived).

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Snopes readers sent emails and searched the website wondering whether the story was true.

There's no evidence supporting the rumor that a black bear took a wounded raccoon and a fox to a wildlife center it had previously received treatment at. It was posted online by a page with a history of posting heartwarming — though fictitious — animal stories generated with artificial intelligence tools.

From quantum error correction to emergent gravity: Probing holographic universes at QLab
Story by Science X staff • 1mo • 6 min read


Two magic-enriched HaPPY-codes are coupled through entanglement between corresponding logical qubits. Increasing the inter-code entanglement decreases the effective length in the wormhole analogy. Credit: QLab
Two magic-enriched HaPPY-codes are coupled through entanglement between corresponding logical qubits. Increasing the inter-code entanglement decreases the effective length in the wormhole analogy. Credit: QLab
One of the deepest ambitions in modern physics is understanding how the fabric of space and time could emerge from fundamental quantum degrees of freedom to establish a quantum theory of gravity.

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Astell & Kern A&Ultima SP4000T Portable High-Resolution Digital Audio Player
Astell & Kern A&Ultima SP4000T Portable High-Resolution Digital Audio Player
Audio46
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

In the new paper, "Observation of gravity-like signatures in holographic codes on a quantum computer," posted to the arXiv preprint server, researchers from the National Quantum Laboratory (QLab@UMD), the Duke Quantum Center, the Virginia Tech Center for Quantum Information Science and Engineering, Caltech's Institute for Quantum Information and Matter, IonQ and BlueQubit have successfully simulated toy models of quantum gravity according to the AdS/CFT correspondence. The team, led by Crystal Noel and Charles Cao, includes quantum-computation pioneers John Preskill and Christopher Monroe.


Conceptual and gate-level schematic of (top) an exact stabilizer HaPPY code and (bottom) a magic-enriched HaPPY code. Credit: QLab
Conceptual and gate-level schematic of (top) an exact stabilizer HaPPY code and (bottom) a magic-enriched HaPPY code. Credit: QLab
Using the IonQ Forte ion-trap quantum computer, the study implements the intriguing HaPPY quantum error-correction code, which forms a bridge between quantum computing and quantum gravity. This code simulates a highly simplified model of a universe with a negative cosmological constant that features a so-called bulk-boundary correspondence, in which the properties of a higher-dimensional gravitational universe (bulk) are completely determined by a lower-dimensional quantum system without gravity living on the boundary. The team successfully demonstrated fundamental entanglement properties of these systems.


Two-layer HaPPY encoding network and local circuit primitives. Credit: arXiv2607.12047_fig5. QLab
Two-layer HaPPY encoding network and local circuit primitives. Credit: arXiv2607.12047_fig5. QLab
The theoretical bedrock: What is AdS/CFT?

(2+1)-dimensional anti-de Sitter space is like a stack of hyperbolic disks, each representing the state of the universe at a given time. Credit: Wikipedia
(2+1)-dimensional anti-de Sitter space is like a stack of hyperbolic disks, each representing the state of the universe at a given time. Credit: Wikipedia
To understand the experiment, it helps to explore the framework of the anti-de Sitter/conformal field theory (AdS/CFT) correspondence, which is so far the most successful realization of the holographic principle, an idea in quantum gravity originally proposed by Gerard 't Hooft and promoted by Leonard Susskind.

Related video: Scientists say reality may be 2D - our universe could be the projection (Naked Science)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Naked Science
Scientists say reality may be 2D - our universe could be the projection

Imagine a solid soup can. The inside of the can (the "bulk") contains a universe governed by gravity and a warped spacetime backdrop called anti-de Sitter space (AdS). Here, the "anti" refers to a negative cosmological constant (Λ < 0), corresponding to an attractive gravitational pressure that tries to pull space inward, while our actual universe is characterized by a positive cosmological constant (Λ > 0), corresponding to a repulsive gravitational pressure that leads to an accelerating expansion of the universe. A funny feature of AdS is the "thermos" effect: If you shine a flashlight in AdS, the light beam will curve, reach the boundary of the universe in a finite amount of time and bounce back to you, as it would in a reflective containment vessel.


arXiv2607.12047_fig1. Credit: QLab
arXiv2607.12047_fig1. Credit: QLab
Meanwhile, the flat labels on the outside of the can (the "boundary") contain a lower-dimensional system of quantum particles that has no gravity at all. In the case of AdS/CFT, this is a highly symmetric conformal field theory (CFT), which is invariant under conformal transformations of spacetime, comprising all angle-preserving transformations like translations, rotations and stretching.

Discovering Group Theory: A Transition To Advanced Mathematics (Textbooks In Mathematics)
Discovering Group Theory: A Transition To Advanced Mathematics (Textbooks In Mathematics)
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The AdS/CFT correspondence was first proposed by Juan Maldacena in 1997. It establishes that the bulk and boundary systems are mathematically equivalent descriptions of the same physics. Everything happening inside the bulk universe—including a particle's path as warped by space—is perfectly encoded in the complex quantum entanglement patterns of particles sitting on the boundary surface. Essentially, space and gravity on the inside can be thought of as a holographic projection emerging directly from the entanglement on the outside.

Connection to quantum error correction: The HaPPY code
How do physicists model a continuous universe inside a finite, digital quantum computer? In this study, the researchers used a beautiful connection to a special type of quantum error-correcting code.

To protect quantum information against environmental noise (decoherence), physicists encode each piece of information—a "logical" qubit—into a highly entangled state spread across many physical qubits. Because the information is encoded in a redundant, distributed way, the system can suffer local damage (like a physical qubit losing its state) without losing the overarching logical information, allowing for correction if errors are not too frequent.

Mysticism, Physics, Polarity And Mother Earth: A Physicist's Report On His Mystical Experiences By Werner, Helmut W. By Thriftbooks, Paperback
Mysticism, Physics, Polarity And Mother Earth: A Physicist's Report On His Mystical Experiences By Werner, Helmut W. By Thriftbooks, Paperback
Thriftbooks.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Remarkably, this computer science concept is also realized by the AdS/CFT correspondence. In this framework, recovering physical events and information encoded deep inside the gravitational bulk from the boundary is fundamentally the same mathematical process as recovering a logical qubit from a network of perturbed physical qubits.

To study the duality in finite quantum systems, researchers use holographic error-correcting codes, with the paradigmatic example being the HaPPY code, named after its creators Harlow, Pastawski, Preskill and Yoshida. The HaPPY code is constructed using a network of "perfect" tensors, tiling a hyperbolic space to represent a slice of the AdS universe. By mapping logical bulk qubits situated inside this network to physical boundary qubits on its outer edge, the HaPPY code reproduces key features of the holographic dictionary. Most notably, it obeys the Ryu–Takayanagi formula, relating geometric areas in the gravitational bulk to quantum entanglement on the boundary.

Let's say noise or an adversary corrupts a massive chunk of the physical qubits on the boundary of the HaPPY code (erasure error). In a "greedy" algorithm, we can then start at the remaining uncorrupted boundary qubits. If a set of working boundary qubits is connected to one of the perfect tensors inside the network, it can use the mathematical properties of that tensor to "shoot" inward, reclaiming the logical space just behind it. As long as the corrupted boundary region is small enough, the algorithm can push all the way to the center, completely bypassing the error and reconstructing the logical qubits.

Experimental milestone: Testing the Faulkner–Lewkowycz–Maldacena formula
A major entry in the holographic dictionary is the Ryu–Takayanagi formula and its quantum-refined successor, the Faulkner–Lewkowycz–Maldacena formula. These equations state that the entanglement entropy (a measure of quantum information) of a region A on the boundary is directly proportional to a corresponding minimal-area surface γA in the bulk (like a soap film stretching into the bulk), plus the entropy of any quantum matter residing in the bulk volume enclosed by A and γA:

S(A)Boundary = Area(γA) / 4GN + SBulk

Using the IonQ Forte quantum computer, the researchers compiled the layers of a HaPPY code into highly optimized gate sequences. Systematically varying the entanglement (θ) of the simulated bulk qubits, the researchers performed full quantum state tomography to extract the resulting entropies. They confirmed for the first time experimentally that, as the bulk matter grew more entangled, the boundary entropy increased in perfect lockstep, leaving the calculated geometric area term perfectly constant—exactly as the FLM formula predicts for a stable background space.

Injecting 'magic' to simulate space distortions
While the standard HaPPY code acts like a rigid background, actual gravity is dynamic: Matter possesses mass and energy that physically warp the geometry of spacetime (gravitational backreaction). Because the standard HaPPY code is built out of basic Clifford gates, its geometry is entirely rigid and incapable of reacting to changes in matter states.

To introduce an analog of this warping, the team injected non-stabilizer resources—known in quantum information science as "magic." By adding deliberate over- and under-rotations to the quantum gates, the circuit was shifted away from its rigid Clifford constraints.

With magic active, the team measured the proto-area entropy (the difference between boundary and bulk entropies). Remarkably, they observed that the geometric area term was no longer static; instead, it grew as the bulk qubits became more entangled. This is analogous to a gravitational system in which the presence of quantum matter alters the surrounding emergent geometry.

Exploring multi-system connectivity
Finally, the researchers pushed the code boundaries into a double-sided configuration by entangling the logical bulk degrees of freedom across two entirely independent copies of the magic-enriched HaPPY code.

According to the famous ER=EPR conjecture pioneered by Maldacena and Susskind, entangling two black holes creates a physical wormhole bridge connecting their spacetimes. In this exploratory circuit setup, the researchers observed the exact opposite trend of the single-copy code: As the entanglement between the two separate code blocks was turned up, the measured proto-area entropy decreased. In holographic language, this drop in entropy is qualitatively reminiscent of a wormhole mouth contracting and shortening the distance between two distinct regions of space.

A note of caution
As the authors emphasize, these results are signatures and analogs for low dimensions, calculated after discretization within a microscopic quantum processor. Because these toy models are limited to a handful of qubits, they also operate in an "N=1" regime—implying that they do not possess a true, smooth semiclassical spacetime background, which only emerges when dealing with thousands or millions of highly complex quantum degrees of freedom. Finally, the study addresses anti-de Sitter space, which features a negative cosmological constant Λ < 0. Extending the holographic principle to the case Λ > 0, as in our actual expanding universe, remains one of the most profound and active open challenges in modern string theory and quantum gravity.

More information: Debopriyo Biswas et al, Observation of gravity-like signatures in holographic codes on a quantum computer, arXiv (2026). DOI: 10.48550/arxiv.2607.12047

Provided by Duke University

The US is reportedly running out of long-range missiles. Defense officials disagree
Story by Taija PerryCook • 1mo • 5 min read


This image shows several long-range missiles, including a Precision Strike Missile and an ATACMS missile. Wikimedia Commons, Snopes Illustration.
This image shows several long-range missiles, including a Precision Strike Missile and an ATACMS missile. Wikimedia Commons, Snopes Illustration.

The claim that the U.S. had burned through "virtually all" of its precision strike missiles during its war on Iran stems from an Aug. 4, 2026, report by news outlet Reuters citing anonymous sources "familiar with the data." 
Also on Aug. 4, CNN reported that anonymous "senior U.S. military commanders" said "nearly 80% of interceptors for a key missile defense system" had been depleted.
While the claim circulating on social media is based on real news media reports from reputable outlets, we left the claim unrated because the reports are based on anonymous sources, which Snopes does not rely on for rated fact checks.
Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth denied the CNN report, and Chief Pentagon Spokesperson Sean Parnell told Snopes that "claims of U.S. munition shortages are false" but did not give any concrete numbers.
We asked the Department of Defense how many of these stockpiled munitions remain and have not received a response as of this writing. We will update this story if we learn more.
In August 2026, social media users across multiple platforms claimed the U.S. had burned through "virtually all" of its precision strike missiles during the war on Iran, which began in February 2026.

One X post (archived) with more than 5.4 million views read:

EXCLUSIVE: The U.S. has used "virtually all" of its precision strike missiles during the war in Iran. Stockpiles on ATACMs and Precision Strike Missiles are almost depleted. And we have used about half of our Tomahawks, per new data circulating inside the admin.


We looked into the facts, and found that while the claim is based on real news media reports, we were unable to rate it using the available evidence because Reuters based its reporting on anonymous sources. U.S. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth and a spokesperson for the Pentagon denied rumors of a munitions shortage but did not say how many of these missiles the U.S. had used or how many remain.

Profile Picture


Illustration of a Cornell-developed metasurface designed to control infrared light. Laser pulses can selectively activate regions of the material, allowing researchers to dynamically tune its optical properties. Credit: Shvets Group
Illustration of a Cornell-developed metasurface designed to control infrared light. Laser pulses can selectively activate regions of the material, allowing researchers to dynamically tune its optical properties. Credit: Shvets Group
Using an engineered metasurface that traps light, Cornell researchers have demonstrated a new way to generate strong static magnetic fields without using external magnets or magnetic materials—an approach that could advance spintronics, quantum and photonic computing, and data storage.

Deploy in Microsoft 365. Affordable for Non-Profits & Any Size Engagement.
Deploy in Microsoft 365. Affordable for Non-Profits & Any Size Engagement.
Pervasent
Pervasent
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

In an article published in Advanced Science, Shivaksh Rawat, a Ph.D. candidate working with Gennady Shvets, the J. Preston Levis Professor of Engineering in the School of Applied and Engineering Physics, and Samyobrata Mukherjee, a postdoc in the same group, described how a new technique for light manipulation—the so-called "time interface"—can be used to convert part of an optical wave's energy into static magnetization.

When a light wave experiences a spatial interface—for example, when it travels through air and then water—some of the light is reflected off the surface while the rest is transmitted through it. Similarly, when a light wave experiences a time interface—a sudden change in the optical properties of the propagation medium, such as an increase or decrease in the refractive index—it also produces reflected and transmitted waves.

Related video: Quantum discovery at MIT surprises physicists (WooGlobe)
View on Watch
View on Watch

WooGlobe
Quantum discovery at MIT surprises physicists
MIT physicists have made a surprising discovery inside a quantum
Current Time 
0:02

Duration 
1:07
0
However, a time interface can also excite a static zero-frequency mode of the system; in other words, stopping part of the light's rapidly varying magnetic field and converting it into a stationary magnetic field pattern that remains in place instead of continuing to oscillate.

Turning trapped light into magnetism
To create a time interface, the researchers used a 2D metasurface—a carefully engineered rectangular array of germanium nanostructures designed to trap mid-infrared light. The researchers wondered what would happen if the metasurface were illuminated with an intense, short burst of higher-energy (near-infrared) photons while the mid-infrared light was still inside the structure.

Introduction To Topology And Geometry
Introduction To Topology And Geometry
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Their modeling revealed that the near-infrared light released electrons from the germanium atoms, leaving "electron holes" and many free electrons. The rapid generation of electron-hole pairs by the near-infrared light can create a time-dependent change in the light's refractive index that acts as a time interface for the trapped mid-infrared light.

"We used an approach known as localized free carrier generation, which has advantages over other methods of nanoscale magnetization," Rawat said. "One of the important contributions of our work is that our approach is material agnostic. Any nonmetallic surface will work."

A brief but persistent field
During the time interface, some of the initial energy in the trapped light shifts to new red-shifted light waves, while the rest is trapped as the kinetic energy of circulating free electrons, which support current loops that sustain a persistent magnetic field in the hot spots. In the absence of losses, this magnetization would persist indefinitely, but even under normal conditions, the magnetic field lasted about 300 femtoseconds, or roughly three 10-trillionths of a second. While that seems extraordinarily short, it equals about 20 cycles of the mid-infrared light wave that created it.

Have You Ever Googled Yourself?
Have You Ever Googled Yourself?
PeopleLooker
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"Our work demonstrates a fundamentally new method for creating strong magnetic fields using light rather than conventional magnets," Rawat said. "By rapidly changing the optical properties of an engineered metasurface, we were able to convert part of a passing light wave into a localized magnetic field that remained after the light had passed. We used a more efficient and highly localized approach than many existing methods, and our work helps explain how energy is redistributed in rapidly changing optical materials."

By understanding how light can both transmit information and create magnetism, the work could open new research directions at the intersection of photonics and magnetism. Beyond advancing time-varying photonics, the research could have important implications for spintronics, magnetic data storage, photonic and quantum computing, and precise control of magnetic environments.

More information: Shivaksh Rawat et al, Generation and Enhancement of Persistent Nanoscale Magnetization in All‐Dielectric Metasurfaces by Optically Injected and Localized Free Carriers, Advanced Science (2026). DOI: 10.1002/advs.76635

Provided by Cornell University

Could quantum mechanics mean you never truly die?
Story by AstroKobi • 4mo • 1 min read


Could Quantum Mechanics Mean You Never Truly Die?
Could Quantum Mechanics Mean You Never Truly Die?
Could Quantum Mechanics Mean You Never Truly Die?

Modern physics suggests reality may be far stranger than we imagine. According to the Many-worlds interpretation, every quantum event could split the universe into multiple parallel worlds, each representing a different outcome.

Introduction To Topology And Geometry
Introduction To Topology And Geometry
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

In traditional quantum theory, the wave function collapses when observed, a view known as the Copenhagen interpretation championed by Niels Bohr and Werner Heisenberg. But in 1957, physicist Hugh Everett III proposed that the wave function never collapses. Instead, reality branches into countless parallel universes.

This idea leads to a provocative thought experiment called quantum immortality. If every life-or-death event creates multiple outcomes, there may always be at least one universe where you survive. Your consciousness, some argue, would continue along that surviving branch.

Most physicists treat this concept as speculation rather than proof. Still, the possibility that the universe constantly splits into parallel realities remains one of quantum physics’ most mind-bending ideas.

Don't believe claim Canadian officials are refusing passage to ships bound for 'Lake America'
Story by Rae Deng • 2w • 3 min read


A map of Lake Ontario with text that says, 
A map of Lake Ontario with text that says,

Claim:

As of August 2026, Canadian officials were refusing passage through the Welland Canal to ships that used the name "Lake America" to refer to Lake Ontario in their documentation.

Rating:

Originated as Satire (About this rating?)

In late August 2026, a rumor spread online that claimed Canadian officials were refusing to grant passage through the Welland Canal — which connects lakes Erie and Ontario — to ships that had "Lake America" listed in their documentation, as opposed to "Lake Ontario." 

The rumor circulated after U.S. President Donald Trump renamed Lake Ontario to "Lake America" in the United States. 

Posts on X, Threads, Facebook (screenshott) and Reddit claimed Fox News had reported on the alleged Canadian blockade. Snopes readers contacted us to investigate the claim's legitimacy.


Don't believe claim Canadian officials are refusing passage to ships bound for 'Lake America'
Don't believe claim Canadian officials are refusing passage to ships bound for 'Lake America'
(X user @MykhailoRohoza)

This claim originated as satire. A search of Fox News' archives via its website found no evidence the news outlet had reported on such a claim. 

Searches on DuckDuckGo, Google and Yahoo also found no relevant and credible news articles reporting on this claim as fact. If Canadian officials really were preventing ships using the term "Lake America" in their documentation from crossing the Welland Canal, it would certainly have made news, and search inquiries would have uncovered evidence of it.

The St. Lawrence Seaway Management Corp. oversees ship traffic on the Canadian side of the Welland Canal, which is part of the St. Lawrence Seaway. The Canadian Press reported that Serge Auclair, vice-president of commercial and external relations at the corporation, said vessels are traveling the canal as usual.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

"Seaway operations are continuing normally and have done so throughout the weekend. Vessels are transiting the Welland Canal as usual," Nicole Giroux, a spokesperson for the St. Lawrence Seaway Management Corp., told Snopes.

The earliest version of the claim we found came from an Aug. 28 X post (archived) from a Russian X user listed as "Comedian" on the account's profile (archived). We reached out to the X user to confirm the account originated the claim and will update this story if we hear back.

Could the mathematical shape of the universe solve the cosmological constant problem?
Story by Kevin Stacey • 4mo • 6 min read


The Hubble Space Telescope showed that distant galaxies are moving away from Earth faster than those nearby, a sign that the universe's expansion is increasing at a rate described by the cosmological constant. That value of that constant is one of the great mysteries of modern physics. Credit: NASA.
The Hubble Space Telescope showed that distant galaxies are moving away from Earth faster than those nearby, a sign that the universe's expansion is increasing at a rate described by the cosmological constant. That value of that constant is one of the great mysteries of modern physics. Credit: NASA.
The cosmological constant is the mathematical description of the energy that drives the ever-accelerating expansion of the cosmos. It's also the source of one of the most enduring and confounding problems in modern physics.

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Discover Scholarship Options
Discover Scholarship Options
University of Phoenix
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The constant's observed value is fundamentally at odds with quantum field theory (QFT), the leading theory describing the elementary particles and forces that make up the universe. QFT predicts that quantum fluctuations in the vacuum of space should make the value of the constant enormous—practically infinite. But its observed value is a tiny fraction of that prediction.

Researchers at Brown University have proposed a provocative new answer for why that is.

The scientists show that the math underlying the simplest formulation of quantum gravity bears a striking resemblance to the math describing the quantum Hall effect, an exotic state of matter in which electricity flows with uncanny precision. In the quantum Hall state, electrical conductance is held steady, regardless of any imperfections in the conducting material, by the system's topology—the mathematical "shape" of the quantum state. The researchers show that there's an analogous topology in what's known as the Chern-Simons-Kodama state, a proposed ground state of quantum gravity.

Related video: A radical alternative to the Big Bang is raising alarming questions (Celestium)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Celestium
A radical alternative to the Big Bang is raising alarming questions
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
16:11
0
"What we've shown is that if space-time has this non-trivial topology, then it resolves one of the deadliest problems of the cosmological constant," said study co-author Stephon Alexander, a professor of physics at Brown. "All the quantum perturbations that should blow up the value of the cosmological constant are rendered inert by this topology, which keeps the constant's value stable."

The research, which Alexander co-authored with Brown Theoretical Physics Center colleagues Aaron Hui and Heliudson Bernardo, is published in Physical Review Letters.

The 'ugly' term
The cosmological constant first appeared as a term in the equations describing Einstein's canonical theory of space, time and gravity, known as general relativity. Einstein was forced to introduce the term to make his mathematical universe stable. It represented a repulsive force, present in the vacuum of space, that counteracted the force of gravity and kept the universe from collapsing on itself.

Introduction To Topology And Geometry
Introduction To Topology And Geometry
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

In 1929, however, the cosmological constant was dealt an existential blow. Astronomer Edwin Hubble discovered that the universe was not as stable as Einstein had assumed. Rather than holding static, it was expanding. That discovery allowed Einstein to remove the stabilizing term from his equations, which he did with some relief. He had long viewed it as "ugly" and is purported to have called it his "biggest blunder."

Following Hubble's discovery, the cosmological constant spent about a half-century on the scientific scrap heap. That changed in 1998, however, when scientists discovered that the universe's expansion is not happening at a constant rate; it's accelerating. That discovery once again made the cosmological constant necessary to describe the increasing speed of the universe's expansion.

Not only was Einstein's ugly term back, it was uglier than ever. During the constant's exile, quantum field theory had become the backbone of the Standard Model of particle physics. According to QFT, empty space is not empty at all. Rather, it's a boiling soup of elementary particles constantly popping in and out of existence.

Songer - AI Music Creator
Songer - AI Music Creator
Songer
Songer
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

All that activity should cause the vacuum energy of space—the energy described by the cosmological constant—to be practically infinite. Yet its observed value, which is estimated by the rate of cosmic expansion, is most definitely not infinity. An infinite value would cause the universe to expand far too quickly to allow the formation of things like galaxies, planets or physicists.

Experiments with elementary particles have shown QFT to be among the most precise and successful theories in all of science, which makes its seemingly errant predictions about the cosmological constant all the more puzzling.

Topologically protected
Alexander has spent years studying Chern-Simons-Kodama (CSK) theory, a proposed state of quantum gravity that grows out of quantum field theory. Scientists have yet to settle on a quantum theory of gravity—a theory that explains how gravity works at the tiniest scales—but the CSK state is one of the more straightforward candidates, according to Alexander.

"It's a really conservative approach to quantizing gravity," he said. "This is the approach used by people like Dirac, Schrödinger and Wheeler. It's just good, old-fashioned quantization."

Alexander had been aware of some mathematical similarities between CSK and the math behind the quantum Hall effect, but he wasn't entirely sure what to make of them. That's when he turned to Hui, an assistant professor at Brown who specializes in topological systems like those that emerge in the quantum Hall effect.

"This is the beauty of the Brown Theoretical Physics Center," Alexander said. "We want to be a place where there's a mixing of lots of perspectives, and this is us practicing what we preach—a cosmologist working closely with a condensed matter theorist."

Together, the researchers were able to show that the cosmological constant has a similar "topological protection" in the CSK state as electrical conductivity has in the quantum Hall effect. The quantum Hall effect emerges when electricity flows through very thin materials in the presence of a magnetic field.

Imagine a flat, two-dimensional piece of metal cut into a rectangular strip with an electric current running longways down the strip. Introducing a magnetic field produces a second voltage that runs perpendicular to the original current. This is known as a Hall voltage (named after Edwin Hall, who discovered it).

At room temperature and under relatively weak magnetic fields, the Hall voltage increases linearly as the strength of the magnetic field increases. But at very cold temperatures, where the rules of quantum mechanics dominate, and under very strong magnetic fields, the phenomenon changes.

Rather than increasing linearly with magnetic field strength, the Hall voltage starts to increase in discrete (or quantized) steps and plateaus. The steps and plateaus are incredibly precise and consistent, taking the exact same values regardless of the type of metal used as a conductor or whether there happen to be any imperfections in it.

That precision and consistency arise because of the system's topology. In these extreme conditions, electrons enter a highly correlated state of collective behavior. It's the mathematical structure of that collective state—its topology—that locks the values of the steps and plateaus into place. The system is topologically protected from perturbations from the material and its imperfections, so the steps and plateaus always have the same value.

The researchers show that a very similar topological protection is present in the equations describing the CSK state. Just as the topology of the electron states locks the Hall voltage into place, the topology of space-time itself locks the cosmological constant into place, even in the face of quantum fluctuations in the vacuum of space.

"What we find is that this quantization of the electrical conductance in quantum Hall has an analog with the cosmological constant," Hui said. "It also ends up becoming quantized for topological reasons. There turn out to be constraints in the theory that force the cosmological constant to take certain allowed quantized values."

There's much more work to be done to fully flesh out a topological solution to the cosmological constant problem, Alexander says. But finding a potential solution to the gravitational aspect of the problem is a crucial start. At the very least, he says, the work bolsters the profile of the CSK state as a candidate for a long-sought theory of quantum gravity.

"We took something old, which is this conservative, canonical approach to quantum gravity, and discovered something new that had been there all along," Alexander said. "Now we're working on a bigger picture of how this phenomenon works."

More information: Stephon Alexander et al, Cosmological Constant from Quantum Gravitational θ Vacua and the Gravitational Hall Effect, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/rzz5-p4f4

Provided by Brown University

Across Egypt, massive granite columns, obelisks, and pyramid stones show clear evidence they were quarried directly from deep bedrock. Quarry marks found on Old Kingdom monuments match the same scoop marks seen at the Aswan granite quarry, suggesting a consistent and controlled extraction method. The Bent Pyramid adds another mystery, with its construction showing planning and precision rather than trial and error. When the stone evidence is taken seriously, ancient Egypt’s engineering capabilities look far more advanced than the standard story allows.

Were 163 missing children found in Florida law enforcement operation? What we know
Story by Anna Rascouët-Paz • 2w • 5 min read


Uthmeier, a white man with brown hair and a close-cut beard, gestures as he speaks into a microphone at a podium that reads 
Uthmeier, a white man with brown hair and a close-cut beard, gestures as he speaks into a microphone at a podium that reads

The claim that authorities in Florida rescued 163 missing children is still under investigation pending the release of more detailed information. 
The offices of Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier and Florida Department of Law Enforcement Commissioner Mark Glass said on Aug. 25, 2026, their agencies had made "46 recoveries by the Tampa Bay region, 41 by Miami, 32 by Jacksonville, 21 by Orlando, 12 by Fort Myers, 8 by Pensacola, and 3 by Tallahassee." 
They said victims' ages ranged from 2 months to 17 years. "Many recovered children had experienced various levels of abuse, neglect, exploitation, or exposure to other criminal activity, including human trafficking," Uthmeier's statement read. 
Florida prosecutor Rita Peters said the mix of children included runaways, indicating the authorities had not just targeted a single trafficking ring. Uthmeier announced three arrests had been made and more would come.
Some posts about the operation claimed the children were recovered from underground cages. There was no evidence this was the case.
Snopes will update this report if additional information surfaces. We have reached out to the FDLE seeking, specifically, the exact number of children who were trafficked, the number of children who were abused and sexually abused, the number of children abducted by one parent over custody battles, the number of runaways and whether further arrests were made in the days since the announcement. We await responses to these questions.
In August 2026, a claim spread that 163 missing children had been recovered in a statewide law enforcement operation in Florida.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

The rumor appeared on Facebook and X. One post on X said the rescued children were "in underground cages with duct tape over their mouths from a human trafficking ring in Florida" (archived):


Several Snopes readers emailed, seeking to confirm the veracity of the claim.

Wild new theories claim the entire universe might be a living organism
Opinion by Kade Winslow • 8mo • 6 min read


Wild new theories claim the entire universe might be a living organism
Wild new theories claim the entire universe might be a living organism
The idea that the cosmos might be alive is moving from late-night speculation into serious, if controversial, scientific conversation. Instead of treating life as a rare accident in a dead universe, a growing set of theories asks whether the universe itself behaves like a living system, complete with metabolism-like flows, self-organization and even something resembling awareness. I want to trace how these wild-sounding proposals are starting to reshape debates about physics, biology and what it means to be an observer inside a possibly living whole.

View Our Current Scholarships - 2026 School Scholarships - University of Phoenix®
View Our Current Scholarships - 2026 School Scholarships - University of Phoenix®
phoenix.edu
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

From “What if?” to serious cosmic thought experiment
For decades, the notion of a living universe sat mostly in philosophy and science fiction, but it is now surfacing in mainstream discussions among researchers and enthusiasts. In one widely shared discussion, Jan introduces a simple but disarming prompt: What if the universe is a living thing and galaxies are just parts of its body, a structure that might have been aware of itself all along. Another conversation, also led by Jan, pushes the idea further, arguing that THE UNIVERSE IS ALIVE and asking whether Some kind of external shock, likened to a really big rubber mallet, would even register for such a vast being, a metaphor that captures how tiny our usual scales of damage and change might be inside a cosmic organism.

These conversations are not happening in a vacuum. A separate thread framed by Aug suggests that Our entire known universe could be a single cell of a larger organism made of 100’s of trillions of such “universe cells,” a picture that treats cosmic structures as nested levels in a biological hierarchy. In another speculative leap, Nov introduces The Cosmocell Hypothesis with the explicit question, What If Our a Microscopic Cell Inside a Vast, Living Organism, treating the Big Bang as a kind of cosmic cell division. I see these as more than playful metaphors; they are informal gateways into a set of formal theories that try to give such images mathematical teeth.

Related video: What if the Earth itself was a single living organism? (Crushin' It)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Crushin' It
What if the Earth itself was a single living organism?
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
0:36
0
Biocentrism and the universe that needs observers
One of the most developed attempts to recast the universe as life-centered is Biocentrism, a framework that insists consciousness is not a latecomer but a fundamental ingredient of reality. In this view, space and time are not rigid backdrops but constructs that arise because living observers measure and experience them, so the cosmos is inseparable from the life that perceives it. Robert Lanza has argued that the universe is structured so that life and mind are built into its fabric, a position laid out in detail in his discussion of the biocentric universe, where life creates time, space and the cosmos itself rather than the other way around.

The same core idea is presented to a broader audience as a revolutionary scientific theory proposing that life and consciousness create the universe, challenging traditional physics-based assumptions and even suggesting the fundamental immortality of life, as described in the book Biocentrism. Earlier coverage framed the 21st century as the Century of Biology, arguing that it is fitting for a biological perspective to reshape cosmology and noting that this life-first approach, called biocentrism, tries to solve quantum puzzles by putting observers into the equation. A companion overview emphasizes that Biocentrism builds on quantum physics, promising to Change the Way You See the World Forever by using life-centered principles to tackle some of the biggest puzzles of science, as set out in a summary of Biocentrism. I read these arguments as a direct challenge to the assumption that the universe is fundamentally inert, suggesting instead that without perception, there is in effect no reality and that Nothing has existence unless you, I, or some living creature perceives it, a line of reasoning explored in depth in a reflection that begins with the claim that Without perception, there is no meaningful cosmos at all.

Deals On Best Books About Quantum Physics - Paperback, Hardcover & More - Enhance Your Knowledge
Deals On Best Books About Quantum Physics - Paperback, Hardcover & More - Enhance Your Knowledge
amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

A universe that self-organizes like a body or a brain
Alongside life-first philosophies, a second strand of thinking focuses on how the universe organizes itself, borrowing tools from complexity science and systems theory. Physician and researcher Neil Theise has popularized the idea of a self-organizing universe, explaining in a talk from Sep that it is pretty simple: systems of interacting individuals, whether people walking down the street or ants in an ant colony, spontaneously form patterns without any central controller, a perspective he develops in detail in The Self-Organizing Universe. This kind of bottom-up order is exactly what we see in galaxies, star clusters and planetary systems, which suggests to me that cosmic structure might be better understood as a kind of emergent behavior rather than a static machine.

That intuition is echoed in more formal work on fractal organization. One influential paper presents a self-organizing fractal theory as a universal discovery method potentially applicable to all disciplines studying organizational phenomena, arguing that similar patterns repeat across multiple levels of the organizational hierarchy, from cells to societies to galaxies, as laid out in its Abstract. The full article, available through a public archive, extends this argument across scales and suggests that the same mathematical rules might govern how tissues, ecosystems and even cosmic structures arrange themselves, a claim developed in detail in the broader self-organizing fractal theory. When I put these pieces together, the universe starts to look less like a static container and more like a dynamic organism whose parts co-create larger patterns, much as cells co-create organs and organs co-create bodies.

Have You Ever Googled Yourself?
Have You Ever Googled Yourself?
PeopleLooker
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Neural networks, cosmic cells and a “live” universe
Some physicists are now going further, asking whether the universe might literally operate like a brain or a neural network. In one widely shared summary, a Physicist proposes the idea that the entire universe could be a neural network controlling everything around us, a bold suggestion attributed to Vitaly Vanchurin, a physics professor at the Univ of Minnesota Duluth, whose work aims to unify quantum mechanics and general relativity by modeling the cosmos as a learning system, as described in detail in a post about this neural network model. Another summary of the same discussion notes that the behavior of such a network could reproduce both quantum mechanics near equilibrium and classical mechanics farther from equilibrium, suggesting that what we call physical law might be the large-scale behavior of an underlying learning process, a possibility that is also flagged in a more general post about a physicist who believes the entire universe could be a neural network.

Biologists are also weighing in from their own angle. One detailed review titled The Live Universe, written from a biologist’s perspective, argues that there are striking parallels between how living systems and cosmic structures evolve, while also stressing that Yet we cannot predict where and how they will go from here, because both biology and cosmology deal with open-ended, path-dependent processes, a point developed in a discussion that explicitly contrasts Biology and Astronomy. In that piece, the author notes that in Biology, unlike Astronomy, we can see an entire life cycle, but still struggle to predict emergent behavior, which suggests to me that our difficulty forecasting the fate of galaxies might be less a failure of data and more a sign that we are dealing with something life-like. A separate proposal from nine researchers, introduced with the invitation to Get ready for a lively debate, suggests a new law of nature that links life, stars, planets and minerals by focusing on the persistent emergence of new patterns or configurations, arguing that What we call evolution may extend beyond organisms to the entire cosmos, as outlined in a report on life, stars and. Taken together, these ideas sketch a universe that not only self-organizes but also continually generates novelty, a hallmark of living systems.

Riley Gaines called WNBA fans in Minneapolis a 'bunch of disgruntled lesbians'
Story by Nur Ibrahim • 1w • 3 min read


Oliver Contreras, accessed via Getty Images.
Oliver Contreras, accessed via Getty Images.

Claim:

Former NCAA swimmer Riley Gaines called WNBA fans in Minneapolis a “bunch of disgruntled lesbians.”

Rating:

Correct Attribution (About this rating?)

In September 2026, a claim circulated online that former NCAA swimmer Riley Gaines, who is now a conservative pundit, called Women's National Basketball Association fans in Minneapolis "a bunch of disgruntled lesbians." 

The purported quote spread on X (archived), Facebook and Instagram (archived), along with clips of Gaines. She reportedly said:

I went to the WNBA game in Minneapolis… it was a bunch of disgruntled lesbians. It felt like the entire arena was booing Sophie Cunningham.

Gaines did indeed make the above remarks on Fox News' "The Big Weekend Show" on Sept. 5. As such, we've rated this claim as a correct attribution. 

Gaines was responding to a question from host Tomi Lahren, who asked why the WNBA wouldn't define a "woman." We have reproduced the exchange below (emphasis ours):

Lahren: …but what I find here so interesting is the WNBA with a "W" in their name, they won't define a woman. And I can't figure out if it's because they have players that are currently playing that wouldn't fit that definition … or is it because they don't want to anger what they feel is a large part of their fanbase that is LGBTQ? Or is it a little bit of both? 

Gaines: I'm sure it's a little bit of both. I can tell you. I went to the WNBA game in Minneapolis. Which you can expect … what the fanbase as it pertains to the WNBA in that city looks like. And it was a bunch of, like, disgruntled lesbians. And I think that the WNBA as a league, organizationally they recognize that. It felt like virtually everyone in that arena, first of all, you audibly heard it, anytime Sophie was subbed in, stepped on the court, touched the ball, threw up a shot, her name was announced on the loudspeakers … you heard the entire arena boo. It felt like they had been advised to only put people on the jumbotron who were draped in their trans flag … And the general public knows that is not indicative of real life. 

Expand article logo  Continue reading

The full episode has been archived and the exchange takes place after 2:38 p.m. 

Gaines was referring to Sophie Cunningham, a WNBA player with the Indiana Fever, who has expressed opposition to transgender players' participation in women's sports. Gaines and Cunningham have also been photographed (archived) together.

Editorial Reviews
Review
From the book reviews:

"The book is divided into two parts, each part corresponding to a one semester course. ... This book is an excellent introduction to astrophysics. It can be used as a text for courses on the subject. Problems are included at the end of each chapter with solutions in the back. Also, each chapter ends with a list of references for further study." (Stephen Wollman, zbMATH, Vol. 1302, 2015)
From the Back Cover
This book gives a survey of astrophysics at the advanced undergraduate level.  It originates from a two-semester course sequence at Rutgers University that is meant to appeal not only to astrophysics students but also more broadly to physics and engineering students.  The organization is driven more by physics than by astronomy; in other words, topics are first developed in physics and then applied to astronomical systems that can be investigated, rather than the other way around.

The first half of the book focuses on gravity.  Gravity is the dominant force in many astronomical systems, so a tremendous amount can be learned by studying gravity, motion and mass.  The theme in this part of the book, as well as throughout astrophysics, is using motion to investigate mass.  The goal of Chapters 2-11 is to develop a progressively richer understanding of gravity as it applies to objects ranging from planets and moons to galaxies and the universe as a whole. The second half uses other aspects of physics to address one of the big questions.   While Why are we here? lies beyond the realm of physics, a closely related question is within our reach: How did we get here?  The goal of Chapters 12-21 is to understand the physics behind the  remarkable story of how the Universe, Earth and life were formed. This book assumes familiarity with vector calculus and introductory physics (mechanics, electromagnetism, gas physics and atomic physics); however, all of the physics topics are reviewed as they come up (and vital aspects of vector calculus are reviewed in the Appendix).

This volume is aimed at undergraduate students majoring in astrophysics, physics or engineering.

Credit: Vienna University of Technology
Credit: Vienna University of Technology
An old puzzle in particle physics has been solved: How can quantum field theories be best formulated on a lattice to optimally simulate them on a computer? The answer comes from AI.

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Deals On Best Books About Quantum Physics - Books For Science Lover - Feel the Power Of Words
Deals On Best Books About Quantum Physics - Books For Science Lover - Feel the Power Of Words
amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Quantum field theories are the foundation of modern physics. They tell us how particles behave and how their interactions can be described. However, many complicated questions in particle physics cannot be answered simply with pen and paper, but only through extremely complex quantum field theory computer simulations.

This presents exceptionally complex problems: Quantum field theories can be formulated in different ways on a computer. In principle, all of them yield the same physical predictions—but in radically different ways. Some variants are computationally completely unusable, inaccurate, or inefficient, while others are surprisingly practical. For decades, researchers have been searching for the optimal way to embed quantum theories in computer simulations. Now, a team from TU Wien, together with teams from the U.S. and Switzerland, has shown that artificial intelligence can bring about tremendous progress in this area. Their paper is published in Physical Review Letters.

Related video: Innsbruck researchers improve quantum simulation accuracy (WooGlobe)
View on Watch
View on Watch
WooGlobe
Innsbruck researchers improve quantum simulation accuracy
Video Player is loading.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
1:05
0
In the computer, the whole world is a grid
"If we want to work with quantum field theories on a computer, we have to discretize them. That's actually nothing unusual," says David Müller from the Institute for Theoretical Physics at TU Wien. Every image on a computer screen consists of small, discrete pixels; when calculating the trajectory of a lunar rocket, the calculation is performed in small, discrete time steps.

It's the same in particle physics: A four-dimensional lattice is created, with three spatial dimensions and one time dimension. Each lattice point is stored on the computer, and the quantum field theory dictates how the lattice points influence each other. In this way, it is possible to simulate, for example, what happens during massive particle collisions at CERN, or how matter behaved shortly after the Big Bang.

In quantum field theory, space and time are continuous. When mapping the theories onto a discrete lattice, however, one has certain degrees of freedom: Different lattice theories correspond to the same continuous theory. One must select a variant that promises the greatest computational success. If this is not done, the computer simulation may run into a dead end and fail to find the correct solution within a realistic timeframe.

The LA Recording School - A Division of LA Film School - Music Production Degree Online
The LA Recording School - A Division of LA Film School - Music Production Degree Online
Los Angeles Film School
Los Angeles Film School
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Different scale, same result
An important key to success are so-called fixed-point equations. "There are certain formulations of quantum field theory on a lattice that have a particularly nice property," explains Urs Wenger from the University of Bern. "They ensure that certain properties remain the same, even if we make the lattice coarser or finer. If this is the case, we know: This property is reliable, it also agrees at coarse resolution—i.e., on a wide-mesh grid—with the continuum that would correspond to an infinitely fine grid."

It's a bit like a map that exists at different scales: Not all details will be the same on every version of the map. But some things don't change when the scale changes—for example, which country borders which other country. This means that one can be quite sure that this property, if it is independent of the map scale, is also a property of reality itself.

Astell & Kern A&Ultima SP4000T Portable High-Resolution Digital Audio Player
Astell & Kern A&Ultima SP4000T Portable High-Resolution Digital Audio Player
Audio46
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The success of AI
Even 30 years ago, experiments were conducted to adapt the lattice formulas in this way. However, there are hundreds of thousands of parameters—far too many for a human. "Many people began exploring these concepts three decades ago, but back then, we simply didn't have the technical means," says Kieran Holland from the University of the Pacific. "By joining forces with the team at TU Wien, we were finally able to revisit these long-standing ideas."

To turn this vision into reality, the team has now developed a very special neural network specifically for this purpose. Ready-made AI solutions do not lead to the goal; it was necessary to develop artificial intelligence that, from the outset, guarantees compliance with the physical laws that are specified.

The team has now succeeded in doing this. The result of the work: The action—the crucial physical quantity in such quantum field theories, also known from Planck's "quantum of action"—could be parameterized on a lattice using AI in such a way that even coarse lattices yield remarkably small errors. "We were able to show that this approach opens up a completely new way to simulate complex quantum field theories with manageable computational effort," says Andreas Ipp from TU Wien.

More information: Kieran Holland et al, Machine-Learned Renormalization-Group-Improved Gauge Actions and Classically Perfect Gradient Flows, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/k41k-2pnc

Provided by Vienna University of Technology

This story was originally published on Phys.org.

Introduction to Topology
by Tej Bahadur Singh (Author)  Format: Hardcover
4.7 4.7 out of 5 stars   (8)
See all formats and editions
Topology is a large subject with several branches, broadly categorized as algebraic topology, point-set topology, and geometric topology. Point-set topology is the main language for a broad range of mathematical disciplines, while algebraic topology offers as a powerful tool for studying problems in geometry and numerous other areas of mathematics. This book presents the basic concepts of topology, including virtually all of the traditional topics in point-set topology, as well as elementary topics in algebraic topology such as fundamental groups and covering spaces. It also discusses topological groups and transformation groups. When combined with a working knowledge of analysis and algebra, this book offers a valuable resource for advanced undergraduate and beginning graduate students of mathematics specializing in algebraic topology and harmonic analysis.

Review

Phys.org
One idea, two cosmic mysteries—linking little red dots and globular clusters
Story by Emily Howard • 1mo • 4 min read


A Little Red Dot (left) and globular cluster 47 Tucanae (right). A new paper by UT Austin astronomers suggests that the two may not be distinct objects, but that instead Little Red Dots are globular clusters caught in the process of forming. Credit: NASA, ESA, CSA, STScI, Dale Kocevski/Colby College, ESO
A Little Red Dot (left) and globular cluster 47 Tucanae (right). A new paper by UT Austin astronomers suggests that the two may not be distinct objects, but that instead Little Red Dots are globular clusters caught in the process of forming. Credit: NASA, ESA, CSA, STScI, Dale Kocevski/Colby College, ESO
A new study led by astronomers at The University of Texas at Austin proposes a theory that could solve two astronomical riddles at once: the nature of Little Red Dots and the origin of globular clusters. Rather than representing distinct objects, the study suggests that one may instead be the ancestor of the other: Little Red Dots are, in fact, an early form of globular clusters. The findings are published in The Astrophysical Journal Letters.

View Our Current Scholarships - 2026 School Scholarships - University of Phoenix®
View Our Current Scholarships - 2026 School Scholarships - University of Phoenix®
phoenix.edu
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

First detected by the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) in 2022, Little Red Dots are mysterious objects that appear 600 million years after the Big Bang, only to seemingly disappear 1.5 billion years later. They are compact, luminous and shine with a distinctive combination of red and ultraviolet light.

One theory is that Little Red Dots represent supermassive black holes, enshrouded in dense clouds of gas, that pull young stars into dramatic deaths. This scenario explains many of the objects' signature properties. However, other scenarios could also fit.

For example, an early globular cluster with a supermassive star at its heart would also look a lot like a Little Red Dot.

"These may not be just a strange new JWST population with no connection to the universe around us today," said John Chisholm, an astronomer at UT Austin and lead author on the study. "Instead, Little Red Dots may persist past the early universe, evolving into something relatively familiar."

Related video: Scientists found a 100,000-solar-mass black hole hiding inside something that looks like a star (Insane Curiosity)

Scientists found a 100,000-solar-mass black hole hiding inside something that looks like a star

0
An old mystery in orbit
Globular clusters are dense collections of ancient stars found orbiting galaxies. The Milky Way alone contains around 150 of them. They can host hundreds of thousands or even millions of stars. Although astronomers have studied these objects for well over a century, their origins remain unknown.

"We usually see them after billions of years of evolution, at a time when their massive stars are gone, their gas has been cleared out, and dynamical processes have changed their masses and structures," explained Danielle Berg, an astronomer at UT Austin and co-author on the study. "That makes it very hard to reconstruct the original conditions they formed in."

The stars in globular clusters are all roughly the same age, developing during a burst of stellar activity in the early universe. While astronomers would expect stars from this era to have relatively straightforward chemistry, some clusters show unexpected patterns that are hard to explain. They contain an abundance of helium, nitrogen, sodium and aluminum, while being low in carbon, oxygen and magnesium.

Deals On Best Books About Quantum Physics - Paperback, Hardcover & More - Enhance Your Knowledge
Deals On Best Books About Quantum Physics - Paperback, Hardcover & More - Enhance Your Knowledge
amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"This specific pattern indicates nuclear fusion at very high temperatures, much higher than in the cores of even massive normal stars," said UT Austin's Mike Boylan-Kolchin, a co-author on the study. "A supermassive star is precisely the kind of environment that could produce this combination."

A chemical trail to follow
Such a gigantic star—up to hundreds of thousands of times more massive than our sun—could have formed from a series of stellar collisions early in a globular cluster's life. As stars merged with one another, over and over again, a central supermassive star would eventually form. Though short-lived, this star would be an incredibly powerful chemical furnace, forging material in its core in unusual ways.

Have You Ever Googled Yourself?
Have You Ever Googled Yourself?
PeopleLooker
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"When they die, they would blow that material back out," explained Berg, "seeding the next generation of stars with the chemical fingerprints we still see in globular clusters today."

"In our model," added Chisholm, "the supermassive star that helps make the object look like a Little Red Dot would live for only a short time. Once that star dies, the object may no longer look like a Little Red Dot, even if the cluster itself survives billions of years."

Clues that line up
While chemistry provides a compelling connection between the two objects, additional clues could also link them. For one, the distribution of Little Red Dots in the early universe corresponds with the distribution of globular clusters in the present day. Models of Little Red Dot evolution also show that their mass could readily transform into that of today's globular clusters. What's more, Little Red Dots appear in the universe at roughly the same time the oldest globular clusters are expected to have formed.

"There's no single smoking gun at this point that says Little Red Dots are globular clusters, but it would explain a lot of diverse and surprising observations," said Boylan-Kolchin.

"Little Red Dots could be galaxies, they could involve black holes, or they could be something even more unexpected," continued Chisholm. "Our work shows that forming globular clusters with supermassive stars should be part of that conversation."

More information: John Chisholm et al, Little Red Dots as Globular Clusters in Formation, The Astrophysical Journal Letters (2026). DOI: 10.3847/2041-8213/ae6dae

Provided by University of Texas at Austin

This story was originally published on Phys.org.

Did Grindr crash during 2026 RNC in Dallas? Facts behind outage claim
Story by Joey Esposito • 4d • 4 min read


The Grindr logo, a yellow mask featuring the app's name in yellow text, on a black phone screen background, is overlaid on a background of Republican National Convention attendees. Randon Bell via Getty Images, Thomas Fuller/SOPA Images/LightRocket via Getty Images, illustrated by Snopes
The Grindr logo, a yellow mask featuring the app's name in yellow text, on a black phone screen background, is overlaid on a background of Republican National Convention attendees. Randon Bell via Getty Images, Thomas Fuller/SOPA Images/LightRocket via Getty Images, illustrated by Snopes

The claim that attendees of the 2026 Republican National Convention in Dallas, Texas, caused an outage of popular gay dating app Grindr circulated online in September 2026. 
The rumor stems from incident reports shared on social media, taken from a third-party service called Downdetector during the time of the event. However, the incidents were not isolated to Dallas, and Grindr itself did not report any major outages connected to the event.
A spokesperson for Grindr told Snopes, "Grindr has not experienced recent outages. Third party sources that often make these claims rely on unreliable data such as self-reporting, while Grindr reports outages directly on its status page." The status page showed no outages in August or September 2026. 
While some users may have had trouble with the service on Sept. 9 and 10, 2026, including in and around the area where the RNC was being held, we were unable to independently verify that the reported errors were authentic or that they were caused specifically by RNC attendees. 
With high-stakes midterms looming in November 2026, the Republican National Convention took place in Dallas, Texas, on Sept. 9 and 10, 2026. Rumors spread that the convention's attendees were causing an outage of the popular gay dating app Grindr.

People on social media sites shared a purported screenshot that showed a map depicting hotspots of Grindr outages across the country, with the brightest spot focused on Dallas. 

The popularity of the rumor led many Snopes readers to reach out via email and social media, asking if the claim was true. 



We weren't able to independently verify claims of Grindr outages in Dallas, so we've left this claim unrated. The screenshots appeared to be taken from Downdetector, a third-party service that tracks outages of popular services, and did not show signs of fabrication or misleading editing. 

Expand article logo  Continue reading

According to Downdetector, users did report incidents at the time and location in question, but the incidents reported on Sept. 9 and 10 were not limited to Dallas or even Texas. There was also no way to confirm that the reports reflected real issues with the app, as Downdetector does not require users to submit any proof.

Grindr itself did not report any outages during that time.

2.4M Followers
This newly discovered decagon above Saturn leaves scientists perplexed
Story by Eric Lagatta, USA TODAY • 1w • 4 min read

For decades, scientists have been aware of a massive, strange six-sided formation on Saturn's north pole.

The mystifying hexagon, unlike anything else seen in the solar system, has persisted long enough for four spacecraft to observe it above the gas giant – including the iconic Hubble Space Telescope.

Now, a new enigmatic 10-sided feature known as the decagon has been spotted swirling on the complete opposite side of the ringed planet. The discovery marks the first time that a regular-sided polygonal jet pattern has been observed on Saturn's southern hemisphere.

Start the day smarter. Get all the news you need in your inbox each morning.

Here's what to know about Saturn, as well as the mysterious 10-sided pattern discovered over its south pole.

What to know about Saturn, 2nd largest planet in solar system

USA TODAY
Scientists puzzled by strange decagon above Saturn's south pole
A newly discovered 10-sided feature above Saturn is puzzling scientists.
Current Time 
0:00

Duration 
0:31
0
The sixth planet from the sun, Saturn is of course famed for its rings – composed of billions of small chunks of ice and rock coated in cosmic dust.

While four robotic spacecraft have explored Saturn, it wasn't until the Cassini probe arrived in 2004 that our understanding of the gas giant – the second largest in our solar system behind Jupiter – began to take shape. After more than a decade of studying Saturn's complex system of rings and moons in unprecedented detail, Cassini was deliberately plunged into Saturn's atmosphere in 2017, ending its mission.

Introduction To Topology And Geometry
Introduction To Topology And Geometry
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Saturn's system is also positively teeming with moons. In fact, a whopping 128 of its 274 observed moons were just discovered in March 2025.

The moons range from planet-size Titan to smaller oddities, some of which are strangely shaped like potatoes or ravioli. And at least two of them, Mimas and Enceladus, have recently been in the news as potential harborers of water – a sign that the worlds could be habitable.

NASA's Hubble spots strange shape on Saturn's south pole
Saturn is also home to a newly discovered phenomenon: a giant, 10-sided enigmatic pattern around its south pole.

The formation was first spotted in 2023 by the Hubble Space Telescope, according to a Sept. 2 press release from NASA. Data, though, suggested the strange feature may have first formed earlier and didn't become visible until Saturn's southern hemisphere tilted back into Earth's view.

Observations have shown the pattern has only become more distinct since it's discovery three years ago. That suggests scientists may be witnessing a new phenomenon develop, according to a study published Sept. 2 in the journal Science Advances led by Agustín Sánchez-Lavega, a researcher at the University of the Basque Country in Spain.

“We’ve never seen anything quite like this in Saturn’s southern hemisphere,” study co-author Amy Simon of NASA’s Goddard Space Flight Center in Maryland said in a statement.

What is the decagon over Saturn?
The feature, known as a decagon, is a weather phenomenon that NASA described as an atmospheric wave. Sitting within one of Saturn's powerful jet streams, the decagon is what the agency says is "the first large, persistent, regular-sided pattern observed at Saturn’s southern hemisphere."

Because it extends through multiple layers of the gas giant's atmosphere, the decagon is far from simply a cloud-level feature. It's also massive, with each of its 10 sides measuring more than 10,000 miles long, according to the study.

Decagon is bigger than what twin Voyagers observed on north pole

This view from NASA's Cassini spacecraft taken July 22, 2013 shows the characteristic hexagonal shape of jet stream on Saturn's north pole from approximately 611,000 miles above.
This view from NASA's Cassini spacecraft taken July 22, 2013 shows the characteristic hexagonal shape of jet stream on Saturn's north pole from approximately 611,000 miles above.
© NASA/JPL-Caltech/Space Science Institute
The decagon has already drawn comparisons to another famous geometric feature observed for more than 40 years on the other side of Saturn.

Saturn's hexagon – a cloud pattern about 20,000 miles wide – was first discovered on the planet's north pole using data from flybys conducted by NASA's twin Voyager probes in 1980 and 1981. Later observations from NASA's robotic Cassini spacecraft between 2004-2017 unveiled the jet streams on the six-sided hexagon spanned 20,000 miles across.

USA TODAY

Hubble has also captured the strange feature, suggesting that it has remained stable for at least four decades.

Additional study of the hexagon and now a decagon could help astronomers compare formations on the opposite ends of Saturn and learn more about a gas giant's weather patterns, according to NASA. Further observations from Hubble and the James Webb Space Telescope could also help scientists understand how the decagon formed and how long it will last.

"The northern hexagon has been there every time we’ve looked for more than 40 years," Simon said in her statement. "This feature is different – it appears to be strengthening, giving us the rare opportunity to watch a giant atmospheric pattern develop.”

Eric Lagatta is the Space Connect reporter for the USA TODAY Network. Reach him at elagatta@usatodayco.com

Were 163 missing children found in Florida law enforcement operation? What we know
Story by Anna Rascouët-Paz • 2w • 5 min read


Uthmeier, a white man with brown hair and a close-cut beard, gestures as he speaks into a microphone at a podium that reads 
Uthmeier, a white man with brown hair and a close-cut beard, gestures as he speaks into a microphone at a podium that reads

The claim that authorities in Florida rescued 163 missing children is still under investigation pending the release of more detailed information. 
The offices of Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier and Florida Department of Law Enforcement Commissioner Mark Glass said on Aug. 25, 2026, their agencies had made "46 recoveries by the Tampa Bay region, 41 by Miami, 32 by Jacksonville, 21 by Orlando, 12 by Fort Myers, 8 by Pensacola, and 3 by Tallahassee." 
They said victims' ages ranged from 2 months to 17 years. "Many recovered children had experienced various levels of abuse, neglect, exploitation, or exposure to other criminal activity, including human trafficking," Uthmeier's statement read. 
Florida prosecutor Rita Peters said the mix of children included runaways, indicating the authorities had not just targeted a single trafficking ring. Uthmeier announced three arrests had been made and more would come.
Some posts about the operation claimed the children were recovered from underground cages. There was no evidence this was the case.
Snopes will update this report if additional information surfaces. We have reached out to the FDLE seeking, specifically, the exact number of children who were trafficked, the number of children who were abused and sexually abused, the number of children abducted by one parent over custody battles, the number of runaways and whether further arrests were made in the days since the announcement. We await responses to these questions.
In August 2026, a claim spread that 163 missing children had been recovered in a statewide law enforcement operation in Florida.
